| REQ-FN-025 | Image upload service with per-category validation (Phase 8) | Verified | 100% | EPIC-IRM-001 Stream F; 7 categories with size/format limits ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-09] PASS — server-side size rule proved: a 2.4 MB PNG into 'profiles' (2 MB cap) was rejected with a precise message and the Upload button stayed disabled - validation runs on selection so the file never reaches storage. Format rule: a .txt is dropped by the per-category MIME accept list before FilesChanged fires; a .png offered to the 'cv' picker likewise produced 0 selected-file panels and a disabled Upload. No blogimage rows or files left by any rejection · screenshot: .verify/shots/resume/req-fn-025-oversize-rejected.png** ‖ **[REQ-FN-025] 2026-08-09 (cluster F) — HOST-LEVEL DEFECT FIXED: a freshly uploaded image was written correctly to disk but `GET /uploads/...` returned the Blazor fallback as **text/html** until the app was rebuilt, so every new image was broken in between. Two causes, both addressed in source/TechieBlog/Program.cs. (a) Runtime-written files exist in no build-time asset manifest, so `MapStaticAssets` registers no endpoint for them; (b) the REQ-UI-012 catch-all route then matched the path, and `UseStaticFiles` deliberately stands aside once an endpoint is selected — which is why a rebuild appeared to "fix" it. The catch-all is gone (see REQ-UI-012), and `/uploads` is now served by its own `UseStaticFiles` over a `PhysicalFileProvider` rooted at `<web root>/uploads`, placed BEFORE `app.UseRouting()` so no present or future fallback route can swallow it again. `ServeUnknownFileTypes` stays false, so a user-writable directory cannot be used to hand an arbitrary file type to a browser. Smoke (real upload through /admin/images as the seeded Admin, no rebuild between upload and fetch): the stored URL `/uploads/profiles/profiles-1-<stamp>.png` returned **200, image/png, 96/96 bytes byte-identical** to the file that was uploaded; a missing upload still returns a bare 404 with a zero-length body rather than an HTML page. Smoke row and file were deleted afterwards and `MustChangePassword` on user 1 re-armed. Image-service internals untouched** ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-11] FAIL — DEMOTED from `Verified`.** The upload dialog states **two contradictory size limits for the same upload**: the category line says `Max 2MB, formats: jpg, jpeg, png, webp` while the dropzone advertises `Max size: 10 MB`. An admin dropping a 5MB avatar is accepted by the dropzone and then rejected by the service. The `accept` filter itself is correct (image/jpeg,image/png,image/webp). Screenshot under `tests/.artifacts/verify-admin/`. ‖ **[REQ-FN-025] 2026-08-11 (cluster C) — FIXED, smoke-verified. ROOT CAUSE: the per-category limits were written down FOUR times — `BlogImageService.CategoryConstraintMap`, `ManageImages.CategoryConstraints`, `ImagePicker.CategoryInfo`, and the TrBlazeUI `FileUpload` component's own untouched `MaxFileSize` default of 10 MB. The page never passed `MaxFileSize`, so the dropzone advertised AND enforced 10 MB while the caption above it read `Max 2MB` — two limits for one upload, the larger one accepting files the server was certain to reject. FIX: the limits now live once, in `BlogModels.ImageCategoryRules` (`source/BlogModel/Common/ImageCategoryRules.cs`), the dependency leaf both the engine and the RCL can reach. `BlogImageService` lost its own table and its MIME map and validates against the shared one; `IBlogImageService` gained `GetCategoryRule(category)`; both upload surfaces lost their duplicate tables and now derive the caption, the `accept` filter AND the dropzone's `MaxFileSize` from that one rule. The dropzone's `OnValidationError` is wired so a client-side refusal still reaches the page's error panel. The server remains authoritative — `UploadImageAsync` re-validates and `BufferUploadAsync` still bounds the read stream by the same ceiling. MEASURED BEFORE: caption `Max 2MB` vs dropzone `Max size: 10 MB`. MEASURED AFTER, all seven categories, caption == dropzone == the value in the source file: profiles 2 MB, logos 500 KB, awards 500 KB, icons 200 KB, blog 5 MB, cv 10 MB, general 5 MB; the `accept` filter also tracks the category (profiles `image/jpeg,image/png,image/webp` → icons `image/png,image/svg+xml,image/webp` → cv `application/pdf`). UPLOADS: a 2.2 MB PNG into `profiles` (2 MB cap) was refused with `cluster-c-over.png exceeds 2 MB limit`, the Upload button stayed disabled and `blogimage` stayed at **2 rows**; a 64 KB PNG uploaded, giving row 21 `cluster-c-under.png|profiles|65536` and **3 rows**; row and file deleted afterwards, table back to **2**. VISUAL: dialog clean at 1280 and 390 — 7/7 controls present, none zero-size or off-viewport, no Cancel/Upload overlap, 0 px horizontal scroll. TESTS: `tests/unit/Media/ImageCategoryRuleTests.cs` pins all seven ceilings, asserts the advertised limit equals the enforced one at the exact byte, proves `UploadImageAsync` refuses an oversize payload server-side writing no row, and greps both upload surfaces for a re-typed size literal. **LIBRARY GAP HIT AND WORKED AROUND — TR-067:** TrBlazeUI 2.0.1's popover `Select` inside `DialogContent` flips to `data-state=open` but renders **zero** `[role="option"]` nodes, so the category was unchangeable by mouse or keyboard; the identical component OUTSIDE the dialog on the same page opens with 5 options. Swapped to `NativeSelect`, which needs no popover. Logged as TR-067 in docs/TechieBlog-TrBlazeUI-Feedback.md. · screenshots `tests/.artifacts/cluster-c/fn025-upload-dialog-{1280,390}.png`, `fn025-oversize-rejected.png`; evidence `fn025-advertised-limits.json`, `fn025-upload-evidence.json`, `fn025-render-{1280,390}.json`** ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-14] PASS — re-verified, row CLOSED.** The 2026-08-11 `FAIL` was two contradictory limits for one upload (caption `Max 2MB` above a dropzone advertising `Max size: 10 MB`). Asserted directly on the live dialog: `/admin/images` -> Upload -> every size figure inside `image-upload-dialog` was extracted and de-duplicated — **2 mentions, 1 distinct value, `2 MB`**, which is exactly `profiles` in `BlogModels.ImageCategoryRules`. The assertion is `== 1`, deliberately not `<= 1`: the first version of this check read the CLOSED page, found zero size figures and passed vacuously, which is the same false-clean shape this row exists to punish. Evidence `tests/verify/req-fn-025-upload-limits.spec.ts`, `tests/.artifacts/req-fn-025/limits.json`. | [view](#d-req-fn-025) |

The prerender double-count trap is handled explicitly, three times over: (1) a view can only be attributed while an HTTP request is in flight, which on an InteractiveServer page is the prerender pass ALONE — the interactive pass sees a null HttpContext and returns Success(false) without writing; (2) the repository's conditional `INSERT ... SELECT ... WHERE NOT EXISTS` refuses a second row for the same visitor and post inside the 24h window; (3) a per-instance `trackedPostId` guard absorbs re-renders such as moving between the pages of a paginated article. Smoke proven against psql, table emptied first: postviews 0 → **1** after ONE anonymous page load (a double count would have given 2); a reload by the same visitor left it at 1; the SAME visitor on a second post took total 1→2 with unique unchanged at 1; a FRESH browser context with a different user-agent took total 2→3 AND unique 1→2. 7 new unit tests in `tests/unit/Analytics/PostViewTrackerTests.cs` (all green) pin the same rules, including that a render with no ambient request writes nothing. KNOWN GAP, flagged not fixed: a reader who reaches a post by client-side navigation inside an already-established circuit has no HttpContext and is not counted — closing that needs a CircuitHandler capturing connection metadata in `Program.cs`, which is outside this cluster's files.** ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-11] PASS** — `PostViewCount` populated for 8 posts, every row non-zero on both total and unique; the dashboard surfaces the tracked counts, so this is no longer dead code. | [view](#d-req-fn-034) |

| REQ-FN-049 | Self-hosted captcha — generate, render, validate (Phase 9) | Verified | 100% | [BRD-99] Added 2026-08-06. **.NET BCL only — no third-party package, no external service, no `System.Drawing.Common`.** `CaptchaSvc` draws a 5-char code from an unambiguous 27-char alphabet with `RandomNumberGenerator`, files it in `IMemoryCache` under a 256-bit id with a 5-minute absolute expiry, and burns the entry *before* comparing so a challenge is strictly single-use right or wrong. **[REQ-FN-049] 2026-08-07 — SECURITY DEFECT CLOSED: the expected answer no longer reaches the client in machine-readable form.** The renderer previously emitted each character as the text content of an SVG `<text>` node, so any script could base64-decode the `data:` URI on the `<img>` and read the answer — a prior agent's harness did exactly that. Fixed by replacing `<text>` with **stroke/path vector glyphs**: new `BlogEngine/Common/CaptchaGlyphSet.cs` holds a normalised M/L/Q outline per alphabet character, and `CaptchaSvgRenderer` scales, shears, rotates (baked into the coordinates — **no `transform` attribute**) and per-point jitters each stroke, emits it as an anonymous `<path>`, mixes the glyph paths with a randomised count of visually identical noise paths and **shuffles the whole list (Fisher-Yates over `RandomNumberGenerator`)** so document order carries no left-to-right tell. **Self-smoke 2026-08-07 (headless Playwright chromium + curl, https://localhost:7413, `/newsletters` subscribe card) — PASS:** (1) *adversarial decode* — fetched a live challenge as a bot would, base64-decoded the `<img src>`: **`<text>` = 0, single-character text nodes = 0, `id`/`class`/`data-*` attributes = 0, `font` references = 0, `<path>` = 16**; element census is only `svg`/`rect`/`path`/`circle` and the attribute set is only `d, fill, stroke, stroke-width, stroke-linecap, stroke-linejoin, opacity, cx, cy, r, rx, width, height, viewBox, role, aria-label, xmlns` — nothing that maps to a character. The previous agent's decode-attack (`/>([A-Z0-9])<\/text>/`) now returns the empty string on every challenge. (2) *human still reads it* — screenshot of the rendered image inspected, code read visually as `WEAYF`. (3) *correct answer validates* — typing that human-read `WEAYF` was accepted and wrote the pending `subscriber` row (verified in PostgreSQL). (4) *wrong answer blocks the write and re-challenges* — a separate run with a unique address and answer `XXXXX` returned "That answer did not match the image", issued a **different** image `src`, cleared the answer box and left **zero** rows in `subscriber`. (5) *reload* issues a new challenge; served HTML still contains **0** `data:image/svg` and **0** `<text` (challenge stays in `OnAfterRenderAsync(firstRender)`, never prerendered). (6) `dotnet build TechieBlog.slnx` **0 errors**; unit suite **189/189 pass** (baseline 180 + 9 new), including regression tests `CaptchaMarkupHasNoTextElement`, `CaptchaMarkupHasNoSingleCharacterTextNode`, `CaptchaMarkupVocabularyIsIndependentOfCode` (word set of the payload is identical for two different codes) and `CaptchaGeometryDiffersBetweenRenders`. Accessibility preserved: the SVG keeps `role="img"` + `aria-label="Verification image. Type the characters you see."` (no answer in the label) and the `<img alt>` is unchanged. ⚠ **Residual weaknesses, stated plainly:** the challenge is now an image-recognition problem rather than a parsing problem, so it is (a) OCR/ML-solvable like any visual captcha, and (b) attackable by *geometric* matching — an attacker who rebuilds the glyph table can cluster paths by x-position and fit them, which jitter/shear/rotation/noise make costly but not impossible; and (c) it remains **inaccessible to blind and low-vision users** — no audio or logic alternative exists (out of scope here, should be raised as its own REQ) ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-09] PASS — wrong answer -> inline captcha-error, psql rows 0 before and 0 after (nothing written), a DIFFERENT challenge issued afterwards, answer box cleared - repeated across 3 runs. Same on the subscribe card. Challenge issued only in OnAfterRenderAsync so served HTML carries only a placeholder; rendered SVG was human-readable in screenshots while carrying no machine-readable answer. Correct answers validated on all three surfaces · screenshot: .verify/shots/engage/newsletter-card-1280.png** | [view](#d-req-fn-049) |

Graded (representative): c1 0.006 s / c10 0.015 s / c25 0.030 s / c50 0.054 s / c100 0.109 s — 100 % under 2 s at every level. **Headroom probed: c200 still 100 % under 2 s at 226 req/s; c400 87.8 % under 2 s with 0 failures** — the budget first breaks between c200 and c400, i.e. 2–4× past the required 100. Against the recorded c100 baseline (p50 1.364 s, p95 2.637 s, 86.0 % under 2 s, 62.4 req/s) that is a **7× throughput gain and the elimination of the 14 % breach**. **Throughput spread is wide (~4×) but the ACCEPTANCE metric has ZERO spread — 100 % under 2 s in every run — so this verdict is robust where the recorded 86.6/65.6/55.8/59.4/86.0 % spread was not.** **Debug/Development is a DIFFERENT ANSWER and must not be quoted as the product figure:** c100 over 3 runs gave 100 %/77.0 %/58.4 % under 2 s at 116/56.7/45.1 req/s, degrading monotonically. Cause measured, not inferred: Development writes **61 206 bytes of Serilog per request** vs Production's **124** (494×), and Serilog rolled five 52 MB files (~250 MB) during those three runs. **HARNESS TRAP recorded so nobody re-diagnoses it:** piping host console output into a drvfs-mounted file drove the SAME binary to **5.6 req/s and p50 15.1 s, pinned at ~5.6 req/s at EVERY concurrency level — indistinguishable from the historic "~3.5 req/s plateau" signature.** Discarding the console alone took it to 116 req/s. **Root-cause probes re-run, not inherited:** during c100 `/post` load PostgreSQL held 93 connections with **0–3 ACTIVE** (database never the constraint); `/health` served **1 283 req/s** at p50 0.061 s; total CPU **99–100 %** — the path is now fully CPU-bound, though the load generator itself took ~1.7 of 8 cores, so these throughputs are a FLOOR not a ceiling. `ThreadPool.SetMinThreads` was NOT re-tried, per the rejected-experiment record. **THE 34.0-vs-18.9 ROUND-TRIP DISCREPANCY IS EXPLAINED.** With `log_statement='all'` scoped to the TechieBlog database (reset afterwards, verified `<none>`), a warm `/` render issues **2 real queries** and `/post` **4** (top-level comments, reply comments, a `PostViews` INSERT, a view-count aggregate) — and **every query is preceded by its own `DISCARD ALL` on the same backend PID**, because Npgsql reopens the logical connection per repository call and each reset commits its own transaction. So **`xact_commit` delta = exactly 2 × real queries** (2→4.00, 4→8.00, matching to two decimals). Cluster B's 34.0/15.6 and the recorded 18.9/7.5 differ by 1.80×/2.08× — a constant *multiplier* across two structurally different pages, the signature of a per-connection artefact, not of features landing between the dates (feature creep adds a constant offset, not a constant ratio). Ruled out: cross-database noise (counter filtered to `datname='TechieBlog'`, idle rate 0–0.4 commits/s sampled either side of every loop), feature creep, page-variant differences, `PostViews` growth (12 rows). **NOT PROVEN:** the pre-change harness `test-results-cluster-k/loadharness.py` no longer exists and git is denied, so which counting rule it used is inferred, not verified. **REMAINING LEVER HAS MOVED — it is rendering CPU, not data access.** Isolation sweep at c100: `/health` (no render, no DB) 644 req/s, `/category/programming` 306, `/newsletters` 237, `/` 216, `/post` 193 — `/` issues only 2 queries thanks to REQ-NFR-018 yet still runs at a third of `/health`, so ~3× of the residual per-request cost is Blazor server-side component rendering. Sub-levers, now optimisations rather than blockers: comments are uncached (2 of `/post`'s 4 queries); the `PostViews` INSERT plus `COUNT(*)`/`COUNT(DISTINCT VisitorHash)` aggregate runs on EVERY render — free at 12 rows but an unbounded per-render aggregate, filed as REQ-NFR-034; `No Reset On Close=true` would halve server round trips, safe only if no session state is used. **Conditions:** 8 logical cores, idle box, nothing else running, load average 0.09–0.46 at rest; shared `WinPostgre` container on 5550 never reset or duplicated; measured across the WSL→Windows gateway exactly as every recorded run was, which is a stated caveat since production would not be reached that way; fresh TCP connection per request (no keep-alive), the pessimistic reading; whole-solution builds 0 errors in both Debug and Release. — PRIOR MEASUREMENT FOLLOWS. **[REQ-NFR-001] 2026-08-09 — RE-MEASURED HONESTLY. Page-load half MET with room to spare; ≥100-concurrent half still NOT MET; and this pass produced NO measurable throughput improvement, which is the expected result and is reported as such.** **Method (reproducible):** `test-results-cluster-k/loadharness.py`, graded concurrency 1/10/25/50/100, five requests per worker rotating over six public URLs (`/`, two `/post/{slug}`, `/category/programming`, `/newsletters`, `/search?q=blazor`); TTFB = wall time from issuing the request to the first response body byte; HTTP/1.1 from WSL to the Windows-hosted Kestrel over the WSL gateway (`172.18.144.1:5391`); **Debug build, `ASPNETCORE_ENVIRONMENT=Development`, 8 logical cores, PostgreSQL in the shared `WinPostgre` container on 5550.** A warm-up pass precedes every run so JIT cost is not charged to the load. **Machine conditions, stated because they dominate the result:** this box concurrently ran cluster L's host on :5392 and four of this cluster's own subagents; 1-minute load average across the runs ranged 0.5–1.4. **Page-load half — MET.** Quiet best-of-3 `curl` TTFB: `/` **0.022 s**, `/post/{slug}` **0.029 s**, `/category/programming` **0.020 s**, `/newsletters` **0.013 s** — two orders of magnitude inside the 2 s budget, and far better than the 2026-08-09 verifier's `/ 0.63 s`, `/post 1.35 s`, which were taken while seven sibling verifiers shared the host. **≥100 concurrent — NOT MET.** At c100, 500 requests: p50 **1.364 s**, p95 **2.637 s**, post p50 **1.509 s**, **86.0 %** under 2 s, 62.4 req/s, **0 failures**. Graded: c1 p50 0.018 s, c10 0.066 s, c25 0.150 s, c50 0.556 s (100 % under 2 s), c100 as above. **The recorded "~3.5 req/s at every concurrency level" and "249 of 500 requests time out at c100" are now DISPROVED** — throughput is 42–62 req/s and nothing timed out, which is the REQ-NFR-013 logging fix landing. **No improvement is claimable from this pass, and the numbers say why:** the pre-change binary measured c100 p50 1.714 s / 62.6 % under 2 s, but five further measurements of the *post*-change binary over 40 minutes gave 86.6 / 65.6 / 55.8 / 59.4 / 86.0 % under 2 s — the single pre-change sample sits squarely inside that range, so **run-to-run variance on this shared box exceeds any effect the code change produced.** That is expected: the service-layer async surface added under REQ-NFR-026 has no callers yet, because the ~109 Blazor call sites that would use it live in files cluster L owns this pass. **Root cause now pinned by measurement rather than inference — three independent probes:** (1) during a c100 `/post` load, `pg_stat_activity` held only **22–24 TechieBlog connections with 1–3 ACTIVE** — the database is idle, never the constraint; (2) `/health`, which touches no database and is fully async, served **1 059 req/s at c100 with p50 15 ms** — Kestrel and the thread pool are healthy, so the constraint is specifically the blocking page-render path; (3) total CPU ran **74–79 %** during the `/post` load, so that page is *partly CPU-bound* and not purely thread-starved. Also measured, from `pg_stat_database.xact_commit` deltas over 20 renders: **`/` issues 18.9 database round trips per render and `/post` 7.5** — the home page's figure is an N+1 in the resume components and is a bigger lever than anything in the data-access layer. **A thread-pool floor was tried and REJECTED on the evidence.** `ThreadPool.SetMinThreads` is the textbook mitigation for blocking-I/O starvation, and it made things consistently worse here: arms alternated A/B/A/B/A/B on one binary at c100 — floor 8 (runtime default) p50 **1.379 / 1.736 / 1.816 s**, 65.6 / 55.8 / 59.4 % under 2 s; floor 256 p50 **2.439 / 2.298 / 2.167 s**, 32.0 / 35.6 / 41.8 % under 2 s. Every round agreed, ranges disjoint: 256 runnable threads oversubscribe 8 cores and cannot buy CPU already spent. `Program.cs` therefore keeps the knob **defaulted to 0 (OFF, runtime behaviour unchanged)** with the measurement written into the comment, so a many-core production host can enable it knowingly and nobody repeats the experiment blind. **The real fix remains REQ-NFR-026 stages 3–4** — see that row. PRIOR MEASUREMENT FOLLOWS. **[REQ-NFR-001] 2026-08-08 — LOGGING HALF ONLY. Root cause (1) below is FIXED and measured; the concurrency figure is NOT re-measured and the ≥100-user half of the acceptance is unchanged at NOT MET.** Serilog is no longer hard-wired to `Debug`. `Program.cs` now applies code defaults (Information; Debug only when the host is Development) and then reads the **`Serilog` section of `appsettings.json`**, which wins — per Coding Standards §Logging. `appsettings.json` pins `Default: Information` with `Microsoft.AspNetCore.Components` and `…SignalR` at `Warning`; the per-component render-tree `Debug` verbosity now lives **only** in `appsettings.Development.json`. **Measured on the booted host, same page (`/`), 100 requests each, counting bytes written to the sink's own file (isolated via `/proc/<pid>/fd`, so concurrently running sibling instances could not contaminate the number):** Development = **65,761 bytes/request** (confirms the ~61 KB/request diagnosis); Production = **144 bytes/request** — a **456× reduction**, i.e. ~58 MB of log for a 955-request run becomes ~140 KB. **NOT re-measured: the concurrency/load figures.** The graded-load numbers below (~3.5 req/s plateau, conc 10 first breach, conc 100 → 249/500 timeouts) stand exactly as recorded on 2026-08-07 and were deliberately NOT re-run — that re-test depends on REQ-NFR-026 stages 3–4, which are not done, and the second root cause (only 41 of 265 public `DbAccess` methods are `async`, so every request blocks a thread-pool thread on Dapper I/O) is untouched and is structural. PRIOR MEASUREMENT FOLLOWS. **[REQ-NFR-001] 2026-08-07 — MEASURED for the first time (headless Chromium + a Node HTTPS load harness against the running app on :7412).** **Page-load half of the acceptance: MET.** Navigation-timing over `/`, `/post/{slug}`, `/resume`, `/newsletters`, `/login`, `/search` at 1280 and 390: TTFB 6–255 ms, **FCP 72–428 ms, load event 53–353 ms — every one of the 12 page/viewport combinations is an order of magnitude inside the 2 s budget.** **Concurrency half: NOT MET.** Graded load (5 requests/worker across 6 public URLs): conc 1 → p50 149 ms, 100 % under 2 s; conc 5 → p50 648 ms, 100 %; **conc 10 → p50 2 354 ms, only 38 % under 2 s (target first breached here)**; conc 25 → p50 5 881 ms; conc 50 → p50 11 182 ms; **conc 100 → 249 of 500 requests TIME OUT, p50 22.9 s, 0.4 % under 2 s.** Throughput pins at **~3.5 req/s at every concurrency level** — the signature of thread-pool starvation, not CPU or database saturation (Postgres held only 16 connections and stayed healthy throughout; the app does NOT recover after the spike and has to be restarted). Two root causes identified, **both outside this cluster's file ownership, neither fixed here**: (1) `source/TechieBlog/Program.cs` hard-codes Serilog `MinimumLevel.Debug()` plus explicit `Override("Microsoft.AspNetCore.Components", Debug)`, writing per-component render-tree logs to console **and** a rolling file with no `IsDevelopment()` gate — measured at **≈61 KB of log per request** (58 MB written during a 955-request run; an earlier session produced a 101 MB / 553 083-line log). (2) Data access is overwhelmingly synchronous — **only 41 of 265 public `DbAccess` methods are `async`** — so each request blocks a thread-pool thread on Dapper I/O. Numbers above are a **Debug build in Development**; a Release re-measure with logging at Information is required before the ≥100-user figure can be called final, but the ~3.5 req/s plateau is structural, not a logging artefact alone. Quiet best-of-3 curl TTFB / 0.63s, /post 1.35s. A Debug or Development number would not be evidence (§4c precondition 1; Development logs ~61 KB/request against Production's ~124 B). Per §4c, `PERF-UNMEASURED` is neither a pass nor a demotion: the row keeps its 2026-08-10 Release/Production measurement and its `Implemented` status, and needs one quiet-host Release/Production run to close. ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-14b] VERIFIED — the quiet-host Release/Production run the row has demanded since 2026-08-07 was finally executed, and BOTH declared budgets pass. All four gates ran.** **The blocker that stopped the two previous attempts was a launch-profile defect, not the salt.** `dotnet run` prints `Using launch settings from …launchSettings.json` and that file pins `ASPNETCORE_ENVIRONMENT=Development`, which **silently overrides an `ASPNETCORE_ENVIRONMENT=Production` set in the environment**. The first boot this pass looked like Production and was not: 87 080 bytes of log in ~4 minutes, full `RenderTree.Renderer` DBG per-component spam. Adding **`--no-launch-profile`** produced a genuine Production host — **0 `RenderTree.Renderer` lines, 1 487 bytes of log** (a ~58× drop on the same binary and the same traffic). Any past "Release/Production" figure taken with a bare `dotnet run` is suspect for this reason. **§4c perf gate — `PERF-OK` on both budgets.** Harness `.tfcore/utils/tf-perf.sh` (never hand-rolled timing), `build_config: Release`, 5 public paths (`/`, two `/post/{slug}`, `/category/programming`, `/newsletters`), warm-up discarded. **Budget 1 — `p95 load <= 2000ms @ concurrency 1`: `PERF-OK`.** p95 **21.8 ms** over 100 samples (0.011× budget), p50 11.1 ms, max 37.5 ms, `weak:false`, 0 errors. **Budget 2 — `p95 load <= 2000ms @ concurrency 100`: `PERF-OK`.** Three independent runs of **10 000 samples each**, `weak:false`, **0 errors, 0 non-200, 0 timeouts in all three (30 000 requests total)**: p95 **2152.5 ms** (cold, 1.076× — `PERF-MARGINAL` band), then **1081.8 ms** (0.541×), then **858.5 ms** (0.429×) on the quiescent host; wall 99.1 s → 51.0 s → 37.8 s. The improvement is monotonic and the ordering is warm-up, so the steady-state figure is the honest one and it is **inside budget with >2× headroom**; the cold first run is recorded as a warning, not a verdict, per the §4c three-band rule. **THIS DECISIVELY REFUTES THE 2026-08-10 §4c RESULT** of "321 of 500 requests timed out (64%), p95 55.9 s, `PERF-FAIL` (load shed)" — that run booted inside WSL on :5099 with a bare `dotnet run`, i.e. almost certainly the Development profile above. Not one request timed out here across 30 000. **§4a render gate — PASS.** All four screens load 200 and render real data: home (4 stat tiles with values, featured post, 3 article cards each with image/category/title/excerpt/author/date/star-rating), post (title, hero, markdown body incl. fenced code, series nav "Part 2 of 3" with prev/next, tags, rating 4.5 from 2 ratings, view-count aggregate, comment form + captcha), category and newsletters. No `#blazor-error-ui`, no console errors. **§4b visual-truth gate — VISUAL-OK at 1280×800 and 390×844**, all 8 combinations: 0 horizontal overflow, no control off-canvas, screenshots inspected (mobile stacks correctly to a hamburger + 2-col stat grid). `tests/verify/req-nfr-001-render-visual.spec.ts` 12/12 passed; shots in `tests/.artifacts/req-nfr-001/`. **Shared-DB cost, disclosed exactly:** a Production boot needs `SiteSettings:BaseUrl` + `Analytics:VisitorSalt`, supplied as **process-scoped environment variables only** (never user-secrets, never a file, nothing persisted). No canonical salt has ever existed on this machine — user secrets hold only `JwtSigningKey`/`AppEncryptionKey` — so nothing was rotated. `PostViews` went **31 rows/9 visitors → 34 rows/11 visitors: 3 rows across the 2 post slugs**, bounded exactly as the one-row-per-visitor-per-post-per-24h dedupe predicts, and consistent with the 31 dev-generated rows already there. **Caveat unchanged from every prior run:** measured across the WSL→Windows gateway, fresh TCP connection per request (no keep-alive, the pessimistic reading); the load generator shares the 8 cores, so these are a floor, not a ceiling. | [view](#d-req-nfr-001) |
| REQ-NFR-002 | Password hashing with an industry-standard salted algorithm (Phase 2) | Verified | 100% | [REQ-NFR-002] 2026-08-07 — **KDF: PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256** (`Rfc2898DeriveBytes.Pbkdf2`, BCL — no third-party package), **210 000 iterations** (OWASP), **128-bit per-user random salt** from `RandomNumberGenerator.GetBytes`, 256-bit subkey, `CryptographicOperations.FixedTimeEquals` for constant-time comparison. Self-describing storage format `PBKDF2-SHA256$<iters>$<b64 salt>$<b64 subkey>` in `BlogModel/Common/PasswordHasher.cs`, so the work factor can be raised later without invalidating credentials. `AppEncrypt.CreateHash` now forwards to it; the retired MD5+fixed-salt digest survives **only** inside `PasswordHasher.VerifyLegacy`. **Migration:** `Verify` returns `SuccessNeedsRehash` for legacy MD5 / legacy plaintext / stale work factor, and `AuthSvc.Authenticate` → `UpgradeStoredHash` re-hashes transparently on the next successful login (failure to persist is logged, never fails the login). ⚠ **Gap found and fixed this pass:** `BlogUI/Pages/AdminPages/AddUser.razor` bypassed all of the above — it hashed with a bare **unsalted SHA256** of its own and accepted any 6-character password, so every admin-created account was both insecure **and unable to ever sign in** (no verifier recognises that hash shape). It now delegates to `AuthSvc.CreateStaffAccount`. **Smoke 2026-08-07 on :5404 — PASS:** all four documented seeded users (Admin/Editor/Author/Contributor) sign in with the UsageGuide passwords; re-hash-on-login proven **directly against PostgreSQL** — contributor's stored value was set to the genuine legacy MD5 digest `b8a42bd…d95b`, one login through the real UI rewrote it to `PBKDF2-SHA256$210000$DSnQUqU136ZnG/clNwX0cg==$…` (32→90 chars, **fresh random salt ≠ the seed salt**, `MustChangePassword` preserved); accounts created through the fixed AddUser page land in the DB as PBKDF2 with distinct random salts. DB restored to the 4 seeded users afterwards. 67/67 security unit tests green; solution build 0 errors ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-09] PASS — VERIFIER-CONFIRMED independently: all four accounts store PBKDF2-SHA256$210000$<b64 salt>$<b64 subkey> (~90 chars) - 210,000 iterations (>=100k), 16-byte salt, 32-byte subkey, and FOUR DISTINCT SALTS so the hash is genuinely per-user salted. No value matches a bare MD5/SHA shape and none is plaintext** | [view](#d-req-nfr-002) |

| REQ-NFR-006 | Input validation + XSS prevention on all user input (Phase 3) | Verified | 100% | **[REQ-NFR-006] 2026-08-07 — Markdown sanitisation audited end-to-end and TWO live stored-XSS holes closed.** **Hole 1 — post/newsletter/resume bodies.** `MarkdownRenderer` used a bare Markdig pipeline; Markdig sanitises nothing, so every author-supplied body was rendered live through `MarkupString`. A 27-vector harness run against the OLD pipeline produced **20 of 27 vectors as executable markup**. Fixed in `BlogEngine/Common/MarkdownRenderer.cs` with three layers: `.DisableHtml()` (raw HTML → escaped text), a new `MarkdownSanitizerExtension` that walks the syntax tree on `DocumentProcessed` and (a) rewrites link/image/autolink destinations failing a scheme allow-list (`http/https/mailto/tel/ftp/ftps`; relative URLs allowed) to `#`, defeating entity-, case- and whitespace-obfuscation, and (b) strips `on*`, `style` and URL-bearing attributes injected via Markdig's generic-attributes extension (`# H {onclick=alert(1)}`). **AFTER: 0 of 27 vectors produce live markup**, while bold/em/code/tables/footnotes/safe links and images still render. **Hole 2 — search excerpts.** `SearchResults.HighlightSearchTerms` injected the raw post abstract into a `MarkupString` unencoded; an abstract containing `<img src=x onerror=…>` executed on the public `/search` page. Now HTML-encodes every segment and weaves in only its own `<mark>` tags. **Comment bodies audited and confirmed already safe** — `CommentThread.razor` renders `@entry.Comment.Comment` as a normal Razor expression (auto-encoded), not Markdown, so the anonymous-comment path was never injectable. **End-to-end proof (not static review):** a probe post + 4 approved anonymous comments carrying `<script>`, `<img onerror>`, `[link](javascript:)`, `<iframe>`, `<svg onload>`, raw-HTML blocks, generic-attribute `onclick`, entity/vbscript/data URLs, `<form action=javascript:>`, `<meta refresh>`, `<style>` and `<object>` were seeded directly in PostgreSQL (never through the UI — the captcha was not weakened), then the rendered pages were loaded in headless Chromium with a dialog listener: **0 dialogs, 0 script/iframe/svg-onload nodes, 0 elements carrying an `on*` handler, 0 `javascript:`/`vbscript:` URLs; all payloads present as escaped text and the safe control content (bold, link, code, table) intact** on BOTH the post-body and comment-body paths. Screenshots retained. Probe fixture deleted from the database afterwards. The defect is real and was removed: `<Authorizing>` was itself a `<LayoutView Layout="MainLayout">`, and `AuthorizeRouteViewCore.RenderContentInDefaultLayout` already wraps BOTH fallback fragments in `LayoutView Layout="DefaultLayout"` — so MainLayout was nested inside MainLayout by construction. The `<NotAuthorized>` twin was worse and nobody had reported it: it wrapped `AccessDenied` in `AuthLayout`, i.e. a nested `<main data-testid="auth-content">` inside `<main data-testid="main-content">` plus a second `ToastProvider`/`PortalHost` pair. Both wrappers are gone. **HONEST RESULT: the long-standing PROJECT-STATUS blocker "prerender->interactive handover shows both shells for ~1.5s" DID NOT REPRODUCE, on the PRISTINE build either.** Sampled every 40 ms through the handover on `/` and on a FULL DOCUMENT LOAD of `/admin` with a live token (280 samples, before and after): `maxHeader=1, maxFooter=1, maxMain=1, emptyTitleSamples=0` in all four runs, and `<Authorizing>` never painted at all (`sawAuthorizing: false`) — the auth state resolves too fast on a local circuit. The `<NotAuthorized>` half is equally unobservable for a different reason: an authenticated-but-unauthorised user is REDIRECTED to the real `/access-denied` route (measured as the seeded contributor against `/admin`, `/settings`, `/users` — all three land on `/access-denied` with exactly 1 `<main>`), so the fragment is not reached. So: the code defect was genuine and is closed, but it should NOT be recorded as the proven root cause of that blocker, and the blocker itself should be re-checked against a real deployment before anyone assumes cluster L closed it. `tests/verify/cluster-l-nested-layout.spec.ts`, `routes-double-shell-{before,after}.jsonl`** ‖ **[REQ-NFR-007] 2026-08-09 (cluster M) — THE RESIDUAL `Tabs` VIOLATIONS ARE CLOSED: the admin area went **48 nodes -> 0**, and with cluster L's public-route result unchanged the WHOLE APPLICATION is now **0 axe nodes** on 16 public + 8 admin routes in BOTH themes. Per route, light and dark identically: `/admin/images` 7->0, `/settings` 5->0, `/comments` 4->0, `/BlogsList` 4->0, `/admin/newsletter` 4->0. Cross-checked by re-running cluster L's own unmodified sweep on this build: **public 0/0 and admin 0/0 in both themes**, keyboard traversal **121 stops, 0 defective** — the same 121 they recorded, so nothing regressed. **THE FIX** is a second MutationObserver in `source/TechieBlog/Components/App.razor`, built on the shape of the TR-052 observer already in that file, with three rules: (a) an `aria-controls` whose target id is NOT in the document is removed — the attribute is asserting something untrue and an absent attribute is strictly better for a screen reader than a dangling one; (b) a `role="tab"` with no `role="tablist"` ancestor loses the role; (c) `aria-selected` is transcribed from the library's own `data-state`. (a) and (b) park the removed value on a `data-` attribute and put it back the moment the DOM makes it true again, so a render batch that lands a trigger before its panel self-heals rather than being permanently stripped. **NEW MEASUREMENT — TR-054's second half was diagnosed WRONG and is now pinned.** The `aria-required-parent` nodes are NOT the composer's nested Tabs: they are the Write/Preview `TabsTrigger` pair inside TrBlazeUI's OWN `MarkdownEditor`, whose list wrapper is a bare `<div>` with no role (`cluster-m-probe.spec.ts` dumps the wrapper and its two children). Injecting `role="tablist"` there was considered and **REJECTED** — arrow keys and Home/End were measured on that pair and do NOT move focus, so a tablist would promise assistive technology a roving-focus contract the widget does not honour. Dropping the unbacked role leaves two correctly-named, operable buttons. Recorded as gap **TR-064**. **NEW DEFECT FOUND AND FIXED, WHICH AXE CANNOT SEE:** the library writes `aria-selected=""` (EMPTY) on the active tab and omits it entirely on the inactive ones; ARIA resolves an empty token to the attribute default, so **NO tab was announced as selected anywhere in the app**. Read from Chrome's own accessibility tree over CDP, not inferred from markup: `/settings` 6 tabs **0** with a `selected` property, `/admin/images` 7 tabs **0** — after the change, **13 tabs, 13 with the state exposed, exactly 1 selected per route**. axe's `aria-valid-attr-value` rule skips empty values, which is why three prior audits passed it. Recorded as gap **TR-063**. **THE TABS STILL WORK — asserted, not assumed,** and the same assertions pass on the PRE-FIX build so they measure the widget and not the fix: `/settings` 6 tabs clicked -> 6 DISTINCT panels, `/admin/images` 7 tabs -> 7 distinct content states (the media library is empty in this database, so the per-category empty-state text is the signature — no smoke run may create content); exactly one tab active after every click and it is the one clicked; `ArrowRight` 0->1, `End` -> last, `Home` -> 0 on both routes with focus never leaving the tablist; a keyboard-arrival focus ring present on the first stop and after the arrow keys (`.focus()` is deliberately NOT used — it does not satisfy `:focus-visible` after a click and would have reported a false failure). Build held at 0 errors and the unit suite at 383/383 throughout. **Why 92% and not 100%:** `source/BlogUI` and `source/TechieBlog` were NOT in scope this pass (2 pre-existing doc warnings remain there — `IUserEventRepo.cs:19` CS1574 unresolvable cref, `MainLayout.razor.cs:1` CS1587), and REQ-NFR-026 stages 3–4 will change signatures the docs describe. **The pass paid for itself in defects, which is the real argument for it:** it found ~20 genuine bugs invisible to the compiler and the test suite, including the REQ-FN-053 resume data-loss defect, `BlogPostRepo.SelectByIdSql`/`SelectBySlugSql` silently discarding `PublishedOn`/`ScheduledPublishOn` (unpublishing erased a post's first-publication date — fixed and counterfactually proved here), four independent defects in the subscriber consent model, `SmtpEmailService` never reading the admin-editable SMTP settings, and `SvcUtils.GetConnectionFromToken` decrypting a connection string out of an unverified JWT. It also corrected documentation that was actively false — two dev email senders inherited "Side Effects: Sends email" via `<inheritdoc/>`, `Subscriber.cs` asserted `IsActive` and `IsConfirmed` were independent when they are the same column, and `BlogTag` named a table that does not exist. | REQ-NFR-018 | Caching layer — settings, taxonomy, listings, output cache (Phase 6) | Verified | 100% | **[REQ-NFR-018] 2026-08-10 — PARTIAL → SMOKE PASS (capped at `Implemented`; no verify-phase run this session). The registered-but-unused service now has a real consumer per named surface.** New `source/BlogEngine/Services/ServiceCache.cs` owns the key vocabulary and invalidation rules; **32 cached read sites and 44 invalidation calls across six services.** *settings* — `SiteSettingsService` stores the effective aggregate under `settings:effective`/`CacheTags.Settings`, replacing a private NO-EXPIRY field cache with a bounded 10-minute lifetime and tag eviction shared with the host's output cache; `InvalidateCache` + `PublishChangeAsync` evict. *taxonomy* — `CategorySvc`, `TagSvc`, `SeriesSvc` listings and by-slug reads under `CacheTags.Taxonomy`, evicted by every create/update/delete and by `TagSvc.GetOrCreateTag` (which inserts). *listings* — `BlogSvc`'s six public reads and `RatingSvc`'s three per-post aggregates under `CacheTags.Content`; all 12 `BlogSvc` write paths call `InvalidateContent`, which evicts content AND taxonomy because a publish moves every category and tag post count. Sync and async twins are cached and invalidated in PAIRS — they cannot share one key (one stores `T`, the other `Task<T>`, and a type mismatch reads as a miss so they would evict each other on every alternating call), so they take adjacent keys under the same tag via `ServiceCache.AsyncVariant`. `ServiceCache.ReadAsync` evicts the key when an awaited read faults, so a momentary database blip cannot park a faulted task and become a 10-minute outage. Deliberately NOT cached, each documented in code: `GetAllPosts` (varies by user/privilege), the scheduled-post queue (the background publisher must see current state), `SearchPosts` (visitor-controlled key space), `GetSinglePost` (admin editor read), every per-email rating read (cross-user disclosure), and `CommentSvc` entirely (a moderator must see the decision they just made). **Measured, `xact_commit` delta over 20 warm renders with background rate sampled either side: `/` 34.0 → 3.6 round trips per render (−89%), `/post/{slug}` 15.6 → 9.15 (−41%).** The `/post` figure is the honest disappointing one — the residual is view tracking, live view counts, comments and the per-visitor rating read, none safely cacheable. **Invalidation proven live, not asserted:** a category renamed through the real admin editor appeared on the very next public render of `/categories`, in both the archive grid and the sidebar, then reverted. Rendered rating aggregates cross-checked against PostgreSQL (4★/2, 4★/2, 5★/4 vs 4.50/2, 4.50/2, 4.75/4). 26 tests in `tests/unit/Caching/CacheConsumerTests.cs` cover hit AND invalidation per surface. **Design note for review:** `ICacheService` is an OPTIONAL trailing constructor parameter (`ICacheService? cacheService = null`) on all six services — DI always injects the registered singleton, and the null path exists so 28 existing `new`-based test call sites kept compiling. Making it required is a mechanical follow-up. ‖ Architecture §8 design; no IMemoryCache registration ‖ 2026-08-07 RECONCILED by *build-phase — row was stale; evidence in the working tree: `AddOutputCache` in Program.cs + `Services/MemoryCacheService.cs` registered as ICacheService (tag-aware). ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-09] PASS — runtime: /sitemap.xml first hit 336ms, second 10ms, byte-identical, response carries age:236 - output cache live. ICacheService/MemoryCacheService singleton backs settings/taxonomy/listings. Gap: the 'output caching for RSS' half has nothing to cache (REQ-FN-037)**  ‖ **[REQ-NFR-018] 2026-08-09 — DEMOTED from `Verified` to `PARTIAL`. The prior verdicts graded REGISTRATION, not USE.** Reported by cluster K during the REQ-NFR-026 async pass and confirmed independently by grep over `source/`: the ONLY references to `ICacheService` outside its own interface and implementation are (a) DI registration in `BlogSvcInitializer.cs` and `MauiProgram.cs`, (b) a sentinel write in `HealthChecks/CriticalServicesHealthCheck.cs`, and (c) two DOC COMMENTS — one of which, in `TagSvc.cs`, says outright "If a caller starts caching tag reads…", i.e. the code itself records that nothing does. **No repository or service caches through it.** So of the four things this REQ names, `output cache` IS real (`AddOutputCache` in Program.cs, and cluster F's new RSS endpoint uses `OutputCachePolicies.Feed` this pass), while `settings`, `taxonomy` and `listings` caching are NOT implemented — a registered-but-unused service. Relevant to REQ-NFR-001: cluster K measured `/` issuing **18.9 DB round trips per render** and `/post` 7.5, so this unused cache is a live performance lever, not a cosmetic gap. Acceptance for closing: at least one real consumer per named surface, with a measured round-trip reduction** ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-11] PASS** — output cache observed working live: `/sitemap.xml` served from cache with a rising `Age` (291 → 496), while the home page and `/healthz` are correctly NoCache. Note the admin verifier independently reported this NOT-OBSERVABLE from a pure black-box view (no cache-status header is exposed); the backend cluster's positive Age-header observation is the stronger evidence and decides the row. | [view](#d-req-nfr-018) |

Cluster D then exercised it at runtime and found the store permanently empty. Two stacked defects, both silent because `RequestPasswordReset` returns the same generic "if an account exists…" response either way (deliberate account-enumeration defence). **(1)** `GetUserByEmail` (script `002`) matched case-sensitively (`WHERE u.EmailId = pLoginMail`) while `AuthSvc.cs:402` lowercases its input, so no account with an upper-case address — including the seeded site owner `Ravi@techieblog.com` — could ever be found; logged only as "Password reset requested for unknown address". **(2)** Past that, `InsertPasswordResetToken` declares `TIMESTAMP` but Npgsql infers `timestamptz` from `DateTime.UtcNow`'s `Kind`, and PostgreSQL resolves overloads strictly → `42883: function insertpasswordresettoken(bigint, text, timestamp with time zone, timestamp with time zone) does not exist`. Fixed by migration `020-CaseInsensitiveEmailLookup.sql` (adopts the `LOWER(...) = LOWER(...)` predicate `017` already uses for the credential lookup, plus a unique functional index) and by normalising the two timestamps' `Kind` in `PasswordResetTokenRepo.InsertToGetId` (setting `DbType` is NOT enough — since Npgsql 6 `DbType.DateTime` itself maps to `timestamptz`). **Runtime evidence, not static:** before → `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM PasswordResetToken` = 0 with `42883` in the log; after → row `tokenid=1, userid=1, createdat=17:14:38, expiresat=+24h, isused=False` and "Password reset token created for user 1". | REQ-UI-057 | Accessible alternative challenge for the self-hosted captcha (Phase 9) | Verified | 100% | [REQ-UI-057] 2026-08-07 — **a text/logic question, not audio.** Audio was rejected deliberately and the reason is on the record: the .NET BCL has **no speech synthesiser**, so a spoken challenge would have meant either a fixed corpus of hand-recorded clips (a small, fingerprintable set — worse security than what it replaces) or a new third-party package, and the acceptance bans the package and explicitly permits a question. New `BlogEngine/Common/CaptchaQuestionSet.cs` + `CaptchaQuestion.cs` draw one of **four shapes** with `RandomNumberGenerator` — word addition, word subtraction, letter-count over a 16-noun bank, word-count over an 8-phrase bank. **Every shape resolves to a NUMBER on purpose:** a multiple-choice question ("which of these is a fruit: cherry, tiger, scarlet?") would print the answer in the page source, which is precisely the defect REQ-FN-049 had to fix for the image. **Same lifecycle as the visual challenge, by construction, not by convention:** `CaptchaSvc.GenerateQuestion()` files its accepted answers through the *same* new `StoreAnswers()` helper as `Generate()`, so both get the same 5-minute absolute expiry and the same burn-before-compare single use, and both are consumed by the same `Validate()`. The cache value became a private `CaptchaAnswerEntry(Kind, AcceptedAnswers)` so a question can accept both "9" and "nine" without loosening the visual path. `CaptchaChallenge` gained `Kind` + `QuestionText`; new `BlogModels.CaptchaChallengeKind`. **UI:** `CaptchaWidget.razor` gained a real focusable `<Button>` — "Can't see the image? Answer a question instead" — in the natural tab order (NOT hover- or pointer-only); in question mode the **question itself is the `<label for>` of the answer box**, so it is the input's accessible name and every screen reader speaks it on focus, and it is *also* pushed through a visually-hidden `role="status" aria-live="polite"` region so switching mode or asking for a new question is announced without focus moving. All three public write surfaces inherit it free — the widget is already mounted on the comment form, the rating step and the subscribe card (REQ-UI-056) — and their captcha-failure routing + card-level wording were made mode-aware. **Cluster H's typed `catch (CaptchaRateLimitedException)` in `IssueChallenge()` was integrated, not reverted** — it sits above my bare catch and my `isQuestionMode` branch feeds both arms. **Keyboard evidence (Playwright Tab/Enter/Space only, zero pointer clicks on the captcha):** toggle reached from page load in **25 Tab stops** on the comment form (`… > comment-input > captcha-reload > captcha-answer > captcha-mode-toggle`), **4** further stops on the rating panel after selecting a star with Space, **5** on the subscribe card; focused element is a real `<button>` with the full sentence as its accessible text. **Answer-leak evidence (the acceptance's hardest clause):** rendered widget text contains **0 digits**; widget markup **0** occurrences of the answer word; **0** page-wide `aria-*`/`data-*`/`title`/`value`/`alt` attributes carry it; **0** occurrences within 400 chars of any captcha marker across **81 websocket frames / 157 KB of decoded blazorpack** (a raw page-wide count is meaningless — the page's own prose may use the word, so the check is scoped to the challenge's own fragment); and the widget markup is **byte-identical across two different challenges** once the question text and auto-generated ids are scrubbed, the DOM-level analogue of REQ-FN-049's `CaptchaMarkupVocabularyIsIndependentOfCode`. The `QuestionHintText` default was rewritten to drop its "for example 9 or nine" worked example — a constant number beside the answer box makes that check unprovable and would one day *be* the answer. **Self-smoke 2026-08-07 (headless Playwright chromium, http://localhost:5406, anonymous, `tests/verify/cluster-g.spec.ts`) — 6/6 PASS:** wrong answer → inline + card error and a **different** question issued (challenge burned); correct answer → **comment accepted** and, on `/newsletters`, the pending subscriber row written; wrong answer on subscribe → "That was not the right answer. Please answer the new verification question and try again." with **no** row written. **VISUAL-TRUTH 1280 + 390 px, screenshots inspected** (`test-results/req-ui-057-{question,image,page}-{1280,390}.png`): question wraps cleanly, toggle focus ring visible, image mode restores both ways, `docOverflow=0` and **`body.scrollWidth === body.clientWidth` (390 === 390)**. ⚠ **Fixed the 272 px mobile overflow routed to me by cluster H** (also flagged on the REQ-FN-023 row): `.tb-keyboard-fallback` hides a `<fieldset>`, and the UA's `min-inline-size: min-content` beats its `width: 1px`, so the "hidden" control was really **622 px wide sitting at right=662** — the post page scrolled sideways for **every mobile visitor**, not just keyboard users, and `clip`/`overflow:hidden` hid the evidence rather than the cause. Two narrow additions to the existing rules in `utilities.css`: `min-width/min-inline-size: 0` on the base rule, and a `> legend` clamp because Chromium's *rendered legend* is laid out over the border box and is therefore **not** clipped by the fieldset's `overflow: hidden` (that second one is why the first pass only moved the number from 272 to 333); plus `flex-wrap/max-width` on the revealed state so the fallback is usable at 390 px. **Before 272 px → after 0 px.** ⚠ Also found and fixed a real crash while smoking: TrBlazeUI `Label` **throws** on a `data-testid` (gap **TR-047**, wrapper workaround applied). **17 new unit tests** in `tests/unit/Engagement/CaptchaQuestionTests.cs` — including `QuestionNeverContainsItsOwnAnswer` and `QuestionTextContainsNoDigits` over **400 generated questions each**, `QuestionChallengeIsSingleUse`, `QuestionBurnsChallengeOnWrongAnswer`, `QuestionChallengeExpires`, `QuestionAndImageChallengesDoNotCrossValidate`. Build **0 errors**; full unit suite **322/322 pass** (3 container integration tests skipped). **Unblocks the 1.1.1 failure that REQ-NFR-007 records; that row is cluster H's re-audit to update, not mine.** ⚠ Residual, stated plainly: a text question is inherently machine-*solvable* (the acceptance requires only that the answer not be *present*, which is proven above) — the rate limiting in REQ-NFR-024 is what makes solving it expensive, and the two were built to work together ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-09] PASS — captcha-mode-toggle is a real focusable <button>; in question mode the question IS the answer box's <label for> accessible name (asserted equal); all four documented shapes solved live; question prose contained 0 digits and the markup contained neither the digit answer nor its number word; toggle present on comment form, rating step and newsletter card · screenshot: .verify/shots/engage/newsletter-card-390.png** | [view](#d-req-ui-057) |
| REQ-NFR-024 | Rate-limit captcha challenge issuance and failures (Phase 9) | Verified | 100% | [BRD-99 ext] Added 2026-08-07 by *build-phase. Single-use + 5-minute expiry stop replay of ONE challenge, but nothing caps how many challenges an IP may request or fail, so the captcha can be brute-forced or used to exhaust server resources. REQ-NFR-005 covers auth endpoints only. Raised by the captcha-hardening pass. ‖ **[REQ-NFR-024] 2026-08-07 — IMPLEMENTED.** Two independent per-client fixed windows, both tunable in the **existing `RateLimiting` section** REQ-NFR-005 established (no second mechanism invented): **issuance `CaptchaIssuePermitLimit` 20 / `CaptchaIssueWindowSeconds` 60**, **failures `CaptchaFailurePermitLimit` 5 / `CaptchaFailureWindowSeconds` 300**. A missing/zero/negative/unparsable setting falls back to the compiled default, so a typo can never switch a security cap off. **Seam:** a decorator, `BlogEngine/Services/RateLimitedCaptchaSvc.cs`, registered as the `ICaptchaService` over the singleton `CaptchaSvc` — every caller (widget, `CommentSvc`, `RatingSvc`, subscribe card) is covered at once and **no path reaches an unlimited captcha**; challenge generation, `CaptchaSvgRenderer` and `CaptchaGlyphSet` are untouched (Cluster G's surface). Counters live in `BlogEngine/Common/CaptchaRateLimiter.cs` (singleton, injectable clock, expired entries dropped on read + bounded sweep so the limiter cannot become its own DoS). **Client key — `CaptchaClientKeyProvider`:** the transport `Connection.RemoteIpAddress`, **never `X-Forwarded-For`** (a client-written header would let an attacker mint a new identity per request, which is worse than no limiter); IPv6 is masked to its **/64** so rotating host bits inside one allocation does not buy a new bucket, and IPv4-mapped IPv6 is unwrapped so one client cannot occupy two. Where no HTTP context is reachable it falls back to a **server-generated per-circuit id** — weaker but unforgeable. Smoke confirmed the IP path is the one actually taken in the circuit (key logged as `captcha-ip:::/64`, and two different circuits shared one bucket). **⚠ FORWARDED-HEADERS FINDING (not silently assumed):** `Program.cs` has **no `UseForwardedHeaders` and no `ForwardedHeadersOptions`** — and REQ-NFR-005's own `AuthRateLimit.BuildPartitionKey` has the same gap. Behind nginx/a load balancer both limiters therefore see the **proxy's** address and the caps become site-wide. Fix belongs at the host, once, for both: `UseForwardedHeaders` with an explicit `KnownProxies`/`KnownNetworks` allow-list. Raised, deliberately not done unilaterally. **Observability:** every trip logs at Warning through `ILogger<T>` with the client key, count, cap and retry-after, tagged `[REQ-NFR-024]`; correlation ids present (REQ-NFR-015). **Self-smoke 2026-08-07 (headless Playwright chromium, http://localhost:5407, `/post/getting-started-with-blazor-server`) — PASS on all four gates:** (1) *under the cap unaffected* — 5 successive reloads produced **5 distinct challenge images, 0 inline errors, 0 page errors**; (2) *issuance cap engages* — refused mid-loop with **"Too many verification attempts. Please wait about 36 seconds and try again."** rendered to the visitor; (3) *failure cap engages* — wrong answers 1–4 kept the captcha's own "The characters did not match." (an ordinary visitor is not punished), attempt **5** flipped to **"Too many verification attempts. Please wait about 5 minutes and try again."** both inline and in the card-level "Your comment was not posted" alert, and a reload while locked out was refused with the same message and **no new image** — i.e. a locked-out client stops growing the answer cache; (4) *self-release* — after the window the challenge was served again with **0 errors**, so it is a rate limit, not a ban. **No spam persisted:** the post still shows **Comments (0)** and no probe address appears in the page. **RENDER-TRUTH:** the rejection is real text in a `role="alert" aria-live="polite"` element, not a blank region. **VISUAL-TRUTH:** screenshots at **1280** and **390** inspected (`test-results/cluster-h/`) — message wraps cleanly, fully inside the viewport at 390 (box x=41 w=308), no clipping. *(A 272 px horizontal overflow at 390 is present on that page but is **not mine** — it is `fieldset[data-testid="post-rating-keyboard"].tb-keyboard-fallback` at right=662, the REQ-NFR-007 keyboard mitigation; reported, not touched.)* **Build 0 errors; unit suite 308 passed / 3 skipped / 0 failed, including 30 new tests** (`tests/unit/Engagement/CaptchaRateLimiterTests.cs`, `RateLimitedCaptchaSvcTests.cs`) covering both caps, **both window resets**, per-client isolation, config binding + misconfiguration fallback, IPv6 /64 masking, and that a blank answer box never spends the failure budget. **⚠ Cross-cluster edit, disclosed:** 5 additive lines in Cluster G's `CaptchaWidget.razor` — a typed `catch (CaptchaRateLimitedException)` above its existing bare `catch` — because that bare catch swallows every exception into a fixed "could not be loaded" string, so a limiter-side refusal would otherwise be **silent**, failing the "clear, accessible wait message" acceptance. Nothing else in the widget was changed. **Residual:** counters are per process, so a multi-instance deployment divides every cap by the instance count — swap in a distributed `ICaptchaRateLimiter` and nothing else changes (same caveat as `LoginThrottle`). ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-09] PASS — static-only + unit tests: RateLimitedCaptchaSvc decorates ICaptchaService over singleton CaptchaSvc with CaptchaRateLimiter; config present (issue 20/60s, failure 5/300s); CaptchaRateLimiterTests + RateLimitedCaptchaSvcTests pass. Not driven at runtime - it keys on client IP shared with 7 sibling verifiers** | [view](#d-req-nfr-024) |
| REQ-NFR-025 | Revoke and rotate the committed GitHub PAT out of nuget.config (Phase 6) | In Progress | 60% | ⚠ SECURITY, OWNER ACTION REQUIRED. Added 2026-08-07 by *build-phase. `nuget.config` at the repo root contains a live GitHub personal access token in `<ClearTextPassword>` for the TrBlazeUI GitHub Packages feed. It is committed, so it is already in git history and rotating the file alone does NOT undo the exposure — the token must be REVOKED on GitHub and reissued. Note `.github/workflows/ci.yml` already refuses to use it and writes a throwaway nuget.ci.config instead. Left in place deliberately during this build so concurrent package restores would not break; removing it will break local restore until the owner supplies credentials via user-secrets or an environment variable. Acceptance: no secret in any committed file; local + CI restore both succeed. ‖ **[VERIFY 2026-08-09] NOT-IMPLEMENTED — OWNER ACTION and the file-side half is definitively NOT done: nuget.config at the repo root STILL contains the live cleartext PAT in <ClearTextPassword> for the TrBlazeUI feed. Revoking on GitHub is outside what any agent can perform. Mitigating: ci.yml refuses to read it and writes a throwaway nuget.ci.config** ‖ **[REQ-NFR-025] 2026-08-09 — FILE-SIDE HALF NOW DONE, and the token is confirmed DEAD. The `<packageSourceCredentials>` block is REMOVED from `nuget.config`; the file now carries only the two `<packageSources>` plus a prominent comment forbidding committed credentials and documenting the user-level `dotnet nuget add source` alternative (which writes outside the repo).
